Or even just forgetting to stop for an errand on the way home. It's a little embarrassing, to be honest, because I'm usually better than that. I just wanted to get it checked out to be safe... does that sound like a good reason to come in?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these memory problems starting to happen? Was it sudden or did it come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart, because I'm really trying my best. I just figured I was a little tired from juggling my job and the classes, which is totally normal, right? I mean, it makes perfect sense that anyone might drop a ball or two with a busier schedule, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Besides the memory and concentration problems, have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past 15 months—such as feeling sad, anxious, or worried more than usual?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Also, have you started taking any new medications or using any substances differently?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / secretary_or_administrative_assistant / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Was it sudden or did it come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart, because I'm really trying my best. But I guess it kind of crept up on me... it's been noticeably harder to manage my organization and keep my attention on all the little details since I started this evening certificate program. \n\nThat was, um, about 15 months ago? I just figured I was a little tired from juggling my job and the classes, which is totally normal, right? I mean, it makes perfect sense that anyone might drop a ball or two with a busier schedule, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Besides the memory and concentration problems, have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past 15 months—such as feeling sad, anxious, or worried more than usual?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm generally a pretty happy person. I don't want you to think I'm depressed or overly anxious or anything like that. I mean, sure, sometimes I feel a little stressed when I realize I forgot something for work, but I think anyone would feel that way, right? It's just normal stress from wanting to do a good job. But as for feeling actually sad or worried all the time... no, nothing like that. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining that okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illnesses, infections, fevers, head injuries, or changes in your physical health around the time these memory problems started? Also, have you started taking any new medications or using any substances differently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, nothing like that at all. Um, I haven't been sick, no fevers, and definitely no head injuries, thank goodness. My physical health has been completely fine. I really try to take good care of myself, you know, so I don't cause any extra issues. \n\nAnd I haven't started any new medications either. As for substances, oh, no, definitely not. I wouldn't do anything like that to jeopardize my job or my classes. So physically, everything is just... normal. I hope that's helpful? It's a good thing that we can rule all of that bad stuff out, right?\n\nDoctor: When you forget things at work and feel stressed about it, do you find yourself feeling down or sad about it afterward, or do you worry that these memory problems might be getting worse and affecting your ability to do your job well?\n\nPatient: Um, I definitely wouldn't say I get sad or down about it. I really try to keep a positive attitude! And honestly, I'm still getting by just fine, I promise. \n\nBut... well, I guess I do worry just a tiny bit about how it looks. It's just that I've missed a few small follow-ups at the office, and, um, a couple of minor deadlines for my classes. Nothing disastrous at all! But it's happened often enough lately that I've started to worry it might, you know, affect my reputation? \n\nI'm really working hard right now because I'd love to move into an Office Manager role eventually, and I just want my boss to see me as completely reliable. That's a pretty normal thing to worry about when you're trying to get a promotion, don't you think? I just really want to make a good impression.\n\nDoctor: Besides worrying about how your memory problems might affect your reputation at work, do you find yourself feeling tense, anxious, or worried about other things in your life—or experiencing physical symptoms like headaches, muscle tension, or difficulty sleeping—even on days when nothing particular has gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. I don't go looking for things to worry about, especially if nothing is actually wrong. I just want to be clear that I'm managing everything else in my life really well. Does that make sense? Um, well... I'd say I really try to leave work at work, you know? When I'm home, I just want to relax and enjoy my weekend. 

I mean, maybe once in a great while, if I'm trying to fall asleep and I suddenly remember an email I forgot to send, I might have a tiny moment of, "Oh gosh, I hope I don't forget that tomorrow." It's really just when I'm right in the middle of a busy workday or trying to finish an assignment for class that I notice it, and even then, I try to just shake it off and keep going. I think it's pretty normal to only think about work stuff when you're actually working, right? I just try to keep a good work-life balance, so I hope that sounds healthy to you.

I mean, I'm generally a pretty happy person. I don't want you to think I'm depressed or overly anxious or anything like that. I mean, sure, sometimes I feel a little stressed when I realize I forgot something for work, but I think anyone would feel that way, right? It's just normal stress from wanting to do a good job. But as for feeling actually sad or worried all the time... no, nothing like that. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining that okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illnesses, infections, fevers, head injuries, or changes in your physical health around the time these memory problems started? Also, have you started taking any new medications or using any substances differently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, nothing like that at all. Um, I haven't been sick, no fevers, and definitely no head injuries, thank goodness. My physical health has been completely fine. I really try to take good care of myself, you know, so I don't cause any extra issues. \n\nAnd I haven't started any new medications either. As for substances, oh, no, definitely not. I wouldn't do anything like that to jeopardize my job or my classes. So physically, everything is just... normal. I hope that's helpful? It's a good thing that we can rule all of that bad stuff out, right?\n\nDoctor: When you forget things at work and feel stressed about it, do you find yourself feeling down or sad about it afterward, or do you worry that these memory problems might be getting worse and affecting your ability to do your job well?\n\nPatient: Um, I definitely wouldn't say I get sad or down about it. I really try to keep a positive attitude! And honestly, I'm still getting by just fine, I promise. \n\nBut... well, I guess I do worry just a tiny bit about how it looks. It's just that I've missed a few small follow-ups at the office, and, um, a couple of minor deadlines for my classes. Nothing disastrous at all! But it's happened often enough lately that I've started to worry it might, you know, affect my reputation? \n\nI'm really working hard right now because I'd love to move into an Office Manager role eventually, and I just want my boss to see me as completely reliable. That's a pretty normal thing to worry about when you're trying to get a promotion, don't you think? I just really want to make a good impression.\n\nDoctor: Besides worrying about how your memory problems might affect your reputation at work, do you find yourself feeling tense, anxious, or worried about other things in your life—or experiencing physical symptoms like headaches, muscle tension, or difficulty sleeping—even on days when nothing particular has gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. I really don't want you to think I'm some sort of bundle of nerves! I'm actually a very relaxed person most of the time. \n\nI don't get tense or, you know, anxious about life in general. And I definitely don't get headaches or muscle tension or anything like that. I sleep just fine, really! I mean, I guess everyone has a bad night's sleep once in a blue moon, but nothing out of the ordinary. \n\nI really try to keep things in perspective, you know? I don't go looking for things to worry about, especially if nothing is actually wrong. I just want to be clear that I'm managing everything else in my life really well. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding defensive, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how things are.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your future—things like your career, your finances, your relationships, or your health—do you find yourself worrying about these things frequently, even when there's no specific reason to worry at that moment?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. But I definitely don't sit around worrying about those things all the time, especially if there's no reason to. \n\nI really try to stay focused on the present and just do my best day to day. I feel like if I work hard now, the future will sort of take care of itself, right? I don't want to be the kind of person who borrows trouble or stresses over things that haven't even happened yet. So, no, I really don't have those kinds of frequent worries. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm explaining things correctly.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home in the evening or on weekends, away from work and your classes, do you find yourself thinking about or worrying about your memory problems and whether they might get worse—or does the worry mostly only come up when you're actually at work or dealing with school tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, well... Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! I still really enjoy the things I've always liked doing. I mean, I love my hobbies and spending time with my friends, and I definitely still look forward to those things.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F48.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me. Um, I hope it's okay that I'm here. It's really nothing too terrible, I just... well, I guess what finally made me ask for help is that I keep forgetting little things that I normally handle without any problem. \n\nLike, I'm a secretary, so I'm used to keeping track of things, but lately I'll just forget to return a phone call, or I'll completely blank on turning in a simple registration form. Or even just forgetting to stop for an errand on the way home. It's a little embarrassing, to be honest, because I'm usually better than that. I just wanted to get it checked out to be safe... does that sound like a good reason to come in?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these memory problems starting to happen? Was it sudden or did it come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart, because I'm really trying my best. But I guess it kind of crept up on me... it's been noticeably harder to manage my organization and keep my attention on all the little details since I started this evening certificate program. \n\nThat was, um, about 15 months ago? I just figured I was a little tired from juggling my job and the classes, which is totally normal, right? I mean, it makes perfect sense that anyone might drop a ball or two with a busier schedule, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Besides the memory and concentration problems, have you noticed any changes in your mood over the past 15 months—such as feeling sad, anxious, or worried more than usual?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm generally a pretty happy person. I don't want you to think I'm depressed or overly anxious or anything like that. I mean, sure, sometimes I feel a little stressed when I realize I forgot something for work, but I think anyone would feel that way, right? It's just normal stress from wanting to do a good job. But as for feeling actually sad or worried all the time... no, nothing like that. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining that okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illnesses, infections, fevers, head injuries, or changes in your physical health around the time these memory problems started? Also, have you started taking any new medications or using any substances differently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, nothing like that at all. Um, I haven't been sick, no fevers, and definitely no head injuries, thank goodness. My physical health has been completely fine. I really try to take good care of myself, you know, so I don't cause any extra issues. \n\nAnd I haven't started any new medications either. As for substances, oh, no, definitely not. I wouldn't do anything like that to jeopardize my job or my classes. So physically, everything is just... normal. I hope that's helpful? It's a good thing that we can rule all of that bad stuff out, right?\n\nDoctor: When you forget things at work and feel stressed about it, do you find yourself feeling down or sad about it afterward, or do you worry that these memory problems might be getting worse and affecting your ability to do your job well?\n\nPatient: Um, I definitely wouldn't say I get sad or down about it. I really try to keep a positive attitude! And honestly, I'm still getting by just fine, I promise. \n\nBut... well, I guess I do worry just a tiny bit about how it looks. It's just that I've missed a few small follow-ups at the office, and, um, a couple of minor deadlines for my classes. Nothing disastrous at all! But it's happened often enough lately that I've started to worry it might, you know, affect my reputation? \n\nI'm really working hard right now because I'd love to move into an Office Manager role eventually, and I just want my boss to see me as completely reliable. That's a pretty normal thing to worry about when you're trying to get a promotion, don't you think? I just really want to make a good impression.\n\nDoctor: Besides worrying about how your memory problems might affect your reputation at work, do you find yourself feeling tense, anxious, or worried about other things in your life—or experiencing physical symptoms like headaches, muscle tension, or difficulty sleeping—even on days when nothing particular has gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. I really don't want you to think I'm some sort of bundle of nerves! I'm actually a very relaxed person most of the time. \n\nI don't get tense or, you know, anxious about life in general. And I definitely don't get headaches or muscle tension or anything like that. I sleep just fine, really! I mean, I guess everyone has a bad night's sleep once in a blue moon, but nothing out of the ordinary. \n\nI really try to keep things in perspective, you know? I don't go looking for things to worry about, especially if nothing is actually wrong. I just want to be clear that I'm managing everything else in my life really well. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding defensive, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how things are.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your future—things like your career, your finances, your relationships, or your health—do you find yourself worrying about these things frequently, even when there's no specific reason to worry at that moment?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. Um, I mean, I care about my future, of course! I think everyone wants to do well in their career and, you know, be financially stable. But I definitely don't sit around worrying about those things all the time, especially if there's no reason to. \n\nI really try to stay focused on the present and just do my best day to day. I feel like if I work hard now, the future will sort of take care of itself, right? I don't want to be the kind of person who borrows trouble or stresses over things that haven't even happened yet. So, no, I really don't have those kinds of frequent worries. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm explaining things correctly.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home in the evening or on weekends, away from work and your classes, do you find yourself thinking about or worrying about your memory problems and whether they might get worse—or does the worry mostly only come up when you're actually at work or dealing with school tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'd say I really try to leave work at work, you know? When I'm home, I just want to relax and enjoy my weekend. \n\nI mean, maybe once in a great while, if I'm trying to fall asleep and I suddenly remember an email I forgot to send, I might have a tiny moment of, \"Oh gosh, I hope I don't forget that tomorrow.\" But it's really just a passing thought! I definitely don't sit on the couch on a Saturday stressing about my memory or anything like that. \n\nIt's really just when I'm right in the middle of a busy workday or trying to finish an assignment for class that I notice it, and even then, I try to just shake it off and keep going. I think it's pretty normal to only think about work stuff when you're actually working, right? I just try to keep a good work-life balance, so I hope that sounds healthy to you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical feelings like a racing heart, sweating, shakiness, or a sense of dread or panic—either when you're worried about something specific at work, or sometimes even when you're not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that at all! I don't get panic attacks or a racing heart or anything extreme like that. \n\nI mean, if I'm running late for something important, maybe my heart beats a little faster for a second, but that's just normal, right? Everyone gets a little flustered sometimes. But I definitely don't get sweaty or shaky, and I've never felt any sort of dread. I'm really a very steady person physically. \n\nI just want to be absolutely clear that I'm not having any kind of severe anxiety or panic issues. I handle stress pretty well, I think. I hope that's a relief to hear? I'd hate for you to think I was falling apart or anything!\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you're taking an evening certificate program while working full-time. When you're in class or studying at home, do you find it hard to focus because your mind keeps drifting to other thoughts or worries, or is it more that you're simply tired and your attention just doesn't feel sharp?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I am definitely a little tired from the long days, which I think is pretty normal for anyone working full-time and taking classes, right? But I wouldn't say I'm sitting there worrying about things. It's more just... little everyday things catching my attention, I guess. \n\nLike, if I'm at the office or in class, just one phone ringing, or hearing people talking nearby... or even just a random thought popping up about something I need to do at home, it can kind of pull me off what I was working on. \n\nI really do try my best to pay attention, I promise! I always want to do a good job. It's just that my mind sort of drifts to those little distractions sometimes instead of staying completely sharp on the material or the task. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information and not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: When your mind drifts away from what you're supposed to be doing, is it usually because of outside noises or things happening around you, or do you find yourself getting stuck on the same worrying thoughts about work or your performance over and over again?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, it's definitely just the outside stuff. Like I said, just a phone ringing or someone walking by. I really don't get stuck on worrying thoughts at all! I'm not an obsessive worrier or anything like that. \n\nI just... my attention just gets caught by whatever is happening around me, you know? It's not like I'm sitting there having these deep, anxious thoughts about my performance. I really try to stay positive and do my best. I hope that's the kind of thing you're looking for? I just want to make sure I'm being clear that I don't have those repetitive, stressful thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over these past 15 months, aside from the memory and concentration issues, have you noticed that you feel less interested or less enjoyment in things you normally enjoy doing, or that you have less energy than you used to?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! I still have plenty of energy to get through my day and do what I need to do. I'm definitely not feeling down or sluggish or anything like that. \n\nI just want to be really clear that I'm doing okay overall, emotionally speaking. I'm a pretty upbeat person! I hope that's helpful? I just don't want you to think I'm depressed or anything, because I'm really not.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something important at work or in class, and your mind drifts away, can you tell me—in that exact moment when it happens—what's actually going through your head? I don't think I'm really thinking about myself at all, to be honest. It's definitely not like I'm sitting there doubting my performance or anything like that! I'm pretty confident in my work, I promise. \n\nIt's really just... like I'll see someone walk past my desk out of the corner of my eye, or I'll hear the office printer start up, and my brain just kind of... follows that instead? I really try to take good care of myself, you know, so I don't cause any extra issues. \n\nAnd I haven't started any new medications either. As for substances, oh, no, definitely not. I wouldn't do anything like that to jeopardize my job or my classes. So physically, everything is just... normal. I hope that's helpful? It's a good thing that we can rule all of that bad stuff out, right?\n\nDoctor: When you forget things at work and feel stressed about it, do you find yourself feeling down or sad about it afterward, or do you worry that these memory problems might be getting worse and affecting your ability to do your job well?\n\nPatient: Um, I definitely wouldn't say I get sad or down about it. I really try to keep a positive attitude! And honestly, I'm still getting by just fine, I promise. \n\nBut... well, I guess I do worry just a tiny bit about how it looks. It's just that I've missed a few small follow-ups at the office, and, um, a couple of minor deadlines for my classes. Nothing disastrous at all! That's a pretty normal thing to worry about when you're trying to get a promotion, don't you think? I just really want to make a good impression.\n\nDoctor: Besides worrying about how your memory problems might affect your reputation at work, do you find yourself feeling tense, anxious, or worried about other things in your life—or experiencing physical symptoms like headaches, muscle tension, or difficulty sleeping—even on days when nothing particular has gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. I don't go looking for things to worry about, especially if nothing is actually wrong. I just want to be clear that I'm managing everything else in my life really well. Does that make sense? But as for feeling actually sad or worried all the time... no, nothing like that. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining that okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent illnesses, infections, fevers, head injuries, or changes in your physical health around the time these memory problems started? Also, have you started taking any new medications or using any substances differently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, nothing like that at all. Um, I haven't been sick, no fevers, and definitely no head injuries, thank goodness. My physical health has been completely fine. I really try to take good care of myself, you know, so I don't cause any extra issues. \n\nAnd I haven't started any new medications either. As for substances, oh, no, definitely not. I wouldn't do anything like that to jeopardize my job or my classes. So physically, everything is just... normal. I hope that's helpful? It's a good thing that we can rule all of that bad stuff out, right?\n\nDoctor: When you forget things at work and feel stressed about it, do you find yourself feeling down or sad about it afterward, or do you worry that these memory problems might be getting worse and affecting your ability to do your job well?\n\nPatient: Um, I definitely wouldn't say I get sad or down about it. I really try to keep a positive attitude! And honestly, I'm still getting by just fine, I promise. \n\nBut... well, I guess I do worry just a tiny bit about how it looks. It's just that I've missed a few small follow-ups at the office, and, um, a couple of minor deadlines for my classes. Nothing disastrous at all! But it's happened often enough lately that I've started to worry it might, you know, affect my reputation? \n\nI'm really working hard right now because I'd love to move into an Office Manager role eventually, and I just want my boss to see me as completely reliable. That's a pretty normal thing to worry about when you're trying to get a promotion, don't you think? I just really want to make a good impression.\n\nDoctor: Besides worrying about how your memory problems might affect your reputation at work, do you find yourself feeling tense, anxious, or worried about other things in your life—or experiencing physical symptoms like headaches, muscle tension, or difficulty sleeping—even on days when nothing particular has gone wrong?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, not at all. I really don't want you to think I'm some sort of bundle of nerves! I'm actually a very relaxed person most of the time. \n\nI don't get tense or, you know, anxious about life in general. And I definitely don't get headaches or muscle tension or anything like that. I sleep just fine, really! I mean, I guess everyone has a bad night's sleep once in a blue moon, but nothing out of the ordinary. \n\nI really try to keep things in perspective, you know? I don't go looking for things to worry about, especially if nothing is actually wrong. I just want to be clear that I'm managing everything else in my life really well. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not sounding defensive, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how things are.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your future—things like your career, your finances, your relationships, or your health—do you find yourself worrying about these things frequently, even when there's no specific reason to worry at that moment?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. Um, I mean, I care about my future, of course! I think everyone wants to do well in their career and, you know, be financially stable. But I definitely don't sit around worrying about those things all the time, especially if there's no reason to. \n\nI really try to stay focused on the present and just do my best day to day. I feel like if I work hard now, the future will sort of take care of itself, right? I don't want to be the kind of person who borrows trouble or stresses over things that haven't even happened yet. So, no, I really don't have those kinds of frequent worries. I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm explaining things correctly.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home in the evening or on weekends, away from work and your classes, do you find yourself thinking about or worrying about your memory problems and whether they might get worse—or does the worry mostly only come up when you're actually at work or dealing with school tasks?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I'd say I really try to leave work at work, you know? When I'm home, I just want to relax and enjoy my weekend. \n\nI mean, maybe once in a great while, if I'm trying to fall asleep and I suddenly remember an email I forgot to send, I might have a tiny moment of, \"Oh gosh, I hope I don't forget that tomorrow.\" But it's really just a passing thought! I definitely don't sit on the couch on a Saturday stressing about my memory or anything like that. \n\nIt's really just when I'm right in the middle of a busy workday or trying to finish an assignment for class that I notice it, and even then, I try to just shake it off and keep going. I think it's pretty normal to only think about work stuff when you're actually working, right? I just try to keep a good work-life balance, so I hope that sounds healthy to you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical feelings like a racing heart, sweating, shakiness, or a sense of dread or panic—either when you're worried about something specific at work, or sometimes even when you're not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that at all! I don't get panic attacks or a racing heart or anything extreme like that. \n\nI mean, if I'm running late for something important, maybe my heart beats a little faster for a second, but that's just normal, right? Everyone gets a little flustered sometimes. But I definitely don't get sweaty or shaky, and I've never felt any sort of dread. I'm really a very steady person physically. \n\nI just want to be absolutely clear that I'm not having any kind of severe anxiety or panic issues. I handle stress pretty well, I think. I hope that's a relief to hear? I'd hate for you to think I was falling apart or anything!\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you're taking an evening certificate program while working full-time. When you're in class or studying at home, do you find it hard to focus because your mind keeps drifting to other thoughts or worries, or is it more that you're simply tired and your attention just doesn't feel sharp?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I am definitely a little tired from the long days, which I think is pretty normal for anyone working full-time and taking classes, right? But I wouldn't say I'm sitting there worrying about things. It's more just... little everyday things catching my attention, I guess. \n\nLike, if I'm at the office or in class, just one phone ringing, or hearing people talking nearby... or even just a random thought popping up about something I need to do at home, it can kind of pull me off what I was working on. \n\nI really do try my best to pay attention, I promise! I always want to do a good job. It's just that my mind sort of drifts to those little distractions sometimes instead of staying completely sharp on the material or the task. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it okay, I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information and not making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: When your mind drifts away from what you're supposed to be doing, is it usually because of outside noises or things happening around you, or do you find yourself getting stuck on the same worrying thoughts about work or your performance over and over again?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, it's definitely just the outside stuff. Like I said, just a phone ringing or someone walking by. I really don't get stuck on worrying thoughts at all! I'm not an obsessive worrier or anything like that. \n\nI just... my attention just gets caught by whatever is happening around me, you know? It's not like I'm sitting there having these deep, anxious thoughts about my performance. I really try to stay positive and do my best. I hope that's the kind of thing you're looking for? I just want to make sure I'm being clear that I don't have those repetitive, stressful thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over these past 15 months, aside from the memory and concentration issues, have you noticed that you feel less interested or less enjoyment in things you normally enjoy doing, or that you have less energy than you used to?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! I still really enjoy the things I've always liked doing. I mean, I love my hobbies and spending time with my friends, and I definitely still look forward to those things. \n\nAnd as for my energy, well... like I mentioned, I get a little tired from working all day and then doing the classes at night, but I think that's just a normal amount of tired, you know? I still have plenty of energy to get through my day and do what I need to do. I'm definitely not feeling down or sluggish or anything like that. \n\nI just want to be really clear that I'm doing okay overall, emotionally speaking. I'm a pretty upbeat person! I hope that's helpful? I just don't want you to think I'm depressed or anything, because I'm really not.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something important at work or in class, and your mind drifts away, can you tell me—in that exact moment when it happens—what's actually going through your head? Are you hearing or seeing something around you that pulls your attention, or are you noticing thoughts about yourself or your performance coming up?\n\nPatient: Um... well, in that exact moment... I don't think I'm really thinking about myself at all, to be honest. It's definitely not like I'm sitting there doubting my performance or anything like that! I'm pretty confident in my work, I promise. \n\nIt's really just... like I'll see someone walk past my desk out of the corner of my eye, or I'll hear the office printer start up, and my brain just kind of... follows that instead? Or maybe my mind just goes a little blank for a second. But there are definitely no negative thoughts about myself or how I'm doing happening when I drift off. \n\nI hope I'm explaining that right? I just want to make sure I'm answering your question properly! Is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: When your mind goes blank or drifts away from your work, and then comes back into focus—what do you notice yourself thinking about in those moments? Is it completely blank, or are there actual thoughts or concerns going through your head, even if they're not about work performance?\n\nPatient: Um... honestly, I think it's mostly just... blank? Or, well, if I am thinking of something, it's just really random, everyday stuff. Like... remembering I need to pick up milk on the way home, or getting a song stuck in my head from the radio. \n\nThere's definitely no deep concerns or anything stressful going on in there, I promise! I'm a pretty happy person, so I'm not sitting there brooding or worrying about things. My brain just sort of... takes a little pause, I guess? And then I realize I'm staring at my computer screen and I just get right back to typing. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I'm trying really hard to give you the right details, so please let me know if I'm answering this the way you need me to!\n\nDoctor: I want to ask you something a bit different. When you look back over these past 15 months, even if you wouldn't describe yourself as sad, have you noticed any change in how you feel about yourself or your worth—like feeling more self-critical, or feeling like you're letting people down, or that things are somehow your fault?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not! I don't feel like that at all. I mean, I know I've been a little scattered with my classes and work, but I don't think I'm a bad person or a failure or anything. \n\nI really try to do my best, and I think the people around me know that. I'm definitely not walking around beating myself up or feeling guilty. Like I said, I try to stay really positive! I know I just need to get a little better organized, that's all. \n\nI hope I'm not sounding defensive? I just want to make sure you know that my self-esteem is totally fine. I'm really okay in that department, I promise! Is that the kind of information you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your mind drifts when you hear a phone or see someone walk by. But I'm curious—when you're sitting quietly at home with no distractions around you, and you're trying to read or study for your classes, do you find it just as hard to concentrate, or does your focus actually stay better when it's quiet?\n\nPatient: Um... you know, I'm actually not entirely sure? I haven't really noticed if it's better or worse, to be completely honest. I guess I just haven't paid close attention to whether my focus changes when it's perfectly quiet. \n\nI mean, I always get my reading done eventually! I'm definitely not falling behind in my classes or anything like that, I promise. But I just... I haven't really tracked if the lack of noise makes a huge difference for me. \n\nI'm so sorry, I wish I had a more specific answer for you! Is it okay that I don't really know? I can definitely start paying more attention to it when I'm at home, if that would be helpful for you!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um, once I catch myself drifting, I can usually get right back to it! I definitely don't just sit there letting my mind wander over and over, I promise.
